## Study plan

Easiest changes first. Related findings share one item, with at most three actions.

1. **Sicilian Defense, move 5: you played 5...e5 in 10 of 21 games; 5...a6 is better** — _Your own positions · 10 minutes a day_

   Also covers: Sicilian Defense, move 5: you played 5...e5 in 6 of 10 games; 5...Nf6 is better · Sicilian Defense, move 6: you played 6...c4 in 5 of 6 games; 6...Nf6 is better

   After 1.e4 c5 2.Nf3 Nc6 3.Nc3 e6 4.d4 cxd4 5.Nxd4 you played 5...e5 in 10 of the 21 games that reached this position. Stockfish analysed one of them: the move cost you about 11 in 100 of your chances to win; Stockfish prefers 5...a6. You go wrong on about 1 move in 7 overall, so playing this one move in 10 of 21 games is a habit, not bad luck.

   - [ ] Set up the position after 1.e4 c5 2.Nf3 Nc6 3.Nc3 e6 4.d4 cxd4 5.Nxd4 and work out why 5...a6 beats 5...e5.
   - [ ] Set up the position after 1.e4 c5 2.Nf3 Nc6 3.Bc4 e6 4.d4 cxd4 5.Nxd4 and work out why 5...Nf6 beats 5...e5.
   - [ ] Solve 10 of your own puzzles a day: 300 positions from your games where your move cost a lot, in bigmuffeater-puzzles.pgn. Import the file into a Lichess study or any chess program.

   _Target for your next report:_ Play 5...a6 the next time this position comes up (you played 5...e5 in 10 of 21 games).

   Review: [Win · 1+1 · vs 591 · 21 Sep](https://www.chess.com/game/live/184164572016) · [Win · 10+0 · vs 1065 · 15 Feb 2025](https://www.chess.com/game/live/122917205436) · [Loss · 1+1 · vs 643 · 24 Sep](https://www.chess.com/game/live/184323574144)

  - Before clicking Rematch, ask whether you want a good game or revenge; if it's revenge, take a break first.

### 1. 2...Nc6, better 2...cxd4

_Repeated mistake · 12% winning chances lost · you played it in 17 games_

- Position: After 1.e4 c5 2.d4. Red: your 2...Nc6, in 17 games (9 bullet, 8 blitz). Green: 2...cxd4, Stockfish's choice. `rnbqkbnr/pp1ppppp/8/2p5/3PP3/8/PPP2PPP/RNBQKBNR b KQkq - 0 2` · [Loss · 3+2 · vs 900 · 31 Jul](https://www.chess.com/game/live/172314741072) · [analyse on Lichess](https://lichess.org/analysis/rnbqkbnr/pp1ppppp/8/2p5/3PP3/8/PPP2PPP/RNBQKBNR_b_KQkq_-_0_2?color=black)
  - Arrows: Your move Nc6 (red), better cxd4 (green)

After 2...Nc6, Stockfish's answer is 3.d5 Nb8 4.f4 g6. Stockfish rates the line −1.53 for you, against −0.19 after 2...cxd4: 3.d5 hits your knight with a pawn and it has to move again (3...Nb8), so you lose time; 2...cxd4 first takes the pawn that chases it. Next time, before you put a piece on a square, check whether a pawn can chase it away with a gain of time.

- What 2...Nc6 allows: `2...Nc6 3.d5 Nb8 4.f4 g6 5.Nc3 Bg7 6.Nf3 d6 7.a4 Bxc3+ 8.bxc3 Nf6 9.Qd3 O-O 10.Be2 … (−1.53 for you)`
- Better: 2...cxd4: `2...cxd4 3.Nf3 e5 4.c3 Nc6 5.cxd4 exd4 6.Nxd4 Nf6 7.Nc3 Bb4 8.Nxc6 bxc6 9.Bd3 d5 10.exd5 … (−0.19 for you)`
- About piece activity: A piece is worth what it can do: how many squares it reaches matters more than its point value. Put your pieces where they have the most scope, and look for ways to cut an enemy piece off from the fight; a bishop or knight shut out of play is almost a piece less. ([Lasker, Chess Strategy (Project Gutenberg \#5614), Chapter III, General Principles of Chess Strategy: Mobility](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/5614))
- 2...Nc6 against 2...cxd4, in pawns: King safety −0.84, Piece activity −0.43, Piece placement −0.33, Space −0.17, Knight placement −0.16, Threats +0.34
- Opening: B21 Sicilian Defense: Smith-Morra Gambit

Next time, ask which squares in your camp no pawn of yours can cover any more, and which enemy piece can reach them.

- you lose the bishop pair
- b6 and d5 become holes in your camp (no pawn of yours can cover them)
- About king safety: Your king is safest behind pawns that haven't moved: each pawn you push in front of it stops guarding squares the enemy pieces can then use for a direct attack, and the pushed pawn itself becomes a target. Castle early, and move those pawns only for a concrete reason, not to stop a pin that isn't there yet. ([Lasker, Chess Strategy (Project Gutenberg \#5614), Chapter VI, The Middle Game: Breaking up the King's Side](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/5614))

After 2...d5, White has a discovered attack: 3.dxe5 dxe4 4.Qxd8+ Kxd8. Stockfish rates the line −1.33 for you, against +0.08 after 2...exd4: 2...exd4 would have won a pawn, and you lose the right to castle. Next time, check what an enemy piece hits when the piece in front of it moves.

- What 2...d5 allows: `2...d5 3.dxe5 dxe4 4.Qxd8+ Kxd8 5.Nc3 Nc6 6.Bf4 Ke8 7.O-O-O Bf5 8.Bc4 Bb4 9.Nge2 Nge7 10.a3 … (−1.33 for you)`

- What 2...d5 allowed: [discovered attack](https://lichess.org/training/discoveredAttack)
- you lose the right to castle
- About king safety: Your king is safest behind pawns that haven't moved: each pawn you push in front of it stops guarding squares the enemy pieces can then use for a direct attack, and the pushed pawn itself becomes a target. Castle early, and move those pawns only for a concrete reason, not to stop a pin that isn't there yet. ([Lasker, Chess Strategy (Project Gutenberg \#5614), Chapter VI, The Middle Game: Breaking up the King's Side](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/5614))
- 2...d5 against 2...exd4, in pawns: King safety −0.45, Piece activity −0.42, Bishop placement +0.25
- Opening: C20 Center Game
  - “White plays 2. d4 to smash open the centre. White threatens to take on e5, but really they are provoking exd4, and planning to develop their pieces rapidly by opening up the d-file for their queen and bishop.” ([Wikibooks, CC BY-SA 4.0](https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Chess_Opening_Theory/1._e4/1...e5/2._d4))

- About open files: Rooks need open files: the side that occupies an open file first can use it to reach the seventh or eighth rank and tie the other side's pieces to defence. When a file opens, get a rook onto it before your opponent does. ([Lasker, Chess Strategy (Project Gutenberg \#5614), Chapter VI, The Middle Game: Manoeuvres of the Pieces, Open Files and Diagonals](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/5614))

Stockfish rates the line −2.19 for you, against −1.38 after 3...Na5: 4.f4 hits your knight with a pawn and it has to move again (4...Ng6), so you lose time, and a few moves later you stand 0.35 pawns worse on pawn structure. Next time, before you put a piece on a square, check whether a pawn can chase it away with a gain of time.

- What 3...Ne5 allows: `3...Ne5 4.f4 Ng6 5.h4 h5 6.Nc3 d6 7.Nf3 a6 8.Bd3 Qb6 9.a4 Bg4 10.a5 Qc7 11.Be2 … (−2.19 for you)`

- About pawn structure: Pawns can't move back, so your pawn structure outlasts almost everything else in the game: an isolated or backward pawn, or doubled pawns that can't protect each other, stay targets long after the moves that made them. Before a pawn move, ask which squares it will stop guarding and whether another pawn can still defend it. ([Lasker, Chess Strategy (Project Gutenberg \#5614), Chapter IV, The Opening: Pawn Skeleton](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/5614))
- 3...Ne5 against 3...Na5, in pawns: Pawn structure −0.35, Space −0.17, King safety +0.15, Rook placement +0.20

- you lose the right to castle
- About king safety: Your king is safest behind pawns that haven't moved: each pawn you push in front of it stops guarding squares the enemy pieces can then use for a direct attack, and the pushed pawn itself becomes a target. Castle early, and move those pawns only for a concrete reason, not to stop a pin that isn't there yet. - About passed pawns: A passed pawn has no enemy pawn in front of it or on the files next to it, so only pieces can stop it, and it grows stronger as pieces come off the board. When your pawns face your opponent's on one side, look for the advance that creates a passed pawn, and count whose pawn queens first. ([Capablanca, Chess Fundamentals (Project Gutenberg \#33870), Chapter II, Further Principles in End-game Play: 11. Obtaining a Passed Pawn](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/33870))
- 5.Bd3 against 5.Qe2, in pawns: Passed pawns −0.43, Piece activity −0.18, Bishop placement −0.15, Threats +0.20

- you get an isolated pawn on d4
- About pawn structure: Pawns can't move back, so your pawn structure outlasts almost everything else in the game: an isolated or backward pawn, or doubled pawns that can't protect each other, stay targets long after the moves that made them. Before a pawn move, ask which squares it will stop guarding and whether another pawn can still defend it. ([Lasker, Chess Strategy (Project Gutenberg \#5614), Chapter IV, The Opening: Pawn Skeleton](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/5614))

- d5 becomes a hole in your camp (no pawn of yours can cover it)
- About king safety: Your king is safest behind pawns that haven't moved: each pawn you push in front of it stops guarding squares the enemy pieces can then use for a direct attack, and the pushed pawn itself becomes a target. Castle early, and move those pawns only for a concrete reason, not to stop a pin that isn't there yet. ([Lasker, Chess Strategy (Project Gutenberg \#5614), Chapter VI, The Middle Game: Breaking up the King's Side](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/5614))
- 5...Qa5 against 5...Nf6, in pawns: King safety −1.47, Piece placement −0.39, Pawn structure −0.17, Queen placement −0.17, Knight placement +0.16, Threats +0.65

- About king safety: Your king is safest behind pawns that haven't moved: each pawn you push in front of it stops guarding squares the enemy pieces can then use for a direct attack, and the pushed pawn itself becomes a target. - About king safety: Your king is safest behind pawns that haven't moved: each pawn you push in front of it stops guarding squares the enemy pieces can then use for a direct attack, and the pushed pawn itself becomes a target. Castle early, and move those pawns only for a concrete reason, not to stop a pin that isn't there yet. ([Lasker, Chess Strategy (Project Gutenberg \#5614), Chapter VI, The Middle Game: Breaking up the King's Side](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/5614))
- 34...Rxg5+ against 34...Qb4, in pawns: Threats −0.59, King safety −0.33, Piece activity −0.21, Passed pawns +0.62

## Why these moves go wrong

The engine's lines for 3 positions from your games: what your move allowed, what was better, and the pattern to practise.

### 1. 5...e5, better 5...Nf6

_Repeated mistake · 11% winning chances lost · you played it in 10 games_

- Position: After … 2...Nc6 3.Nc3 e6 4.d4 cxd4 5.Nxd4. Red: your 5...e5, in 10 games (4 bullet, 6 blitz). Green: 5...Nf6, Stockfish's choice. `r1bqkbnr/pp1p1ppp/2n1p3/8/3NP3/2N5/PPP2PPP/R1BQKB1R b KQkq - 0 5` · [Win · 1+1 · vs 591 · 21 Sep](https://www.chess.com/game/live/184164572016) · [analyse on Lichess](https://lichess.org/analysis/r1bqkbnr/pp1p1ppp/2n1p3/8/3NP3/2N5/PPP2PPP/R1BQKB1R_b_KQkq_-_0_5?color=black)
  - Arrows: Your move e5 (red), better Nf6 (green)

After 5...e5, Stockfish's answer is 6.Ndb5 a6 7.Nd6+ Bxd6. Stockfish rates the line −1.57 for you, against −0.39 after 5...Nf6: White's knight gets to d6 (7.Nd6+), a square no pawn of yours can cover, and a few moves later you stand 1.17 pawns worse on king safety. Next time, ask which squares in your camp no pawn of yours can cover any more, and which enemy piece can reach them.

- What 5...e5 allows: `5...e5 6.Ndb5 a6 7.Nd6+ Bxd6 8.Qxd6 Qe7 9.Qd1 Nf6 10.Bg5 d6 11.Nd5 Qd8 12.Bxf6 gxf6 13.Qf3 … (−1.57 for you)`
- Better: 5...Nf6: `5...Nf6 6.Nxc6 bxc6 7.e5 Nd5 8.Ne4 Qc7 9.f4 Qa5+ 10.Bd2 Qb6 11.Bd3 Nb4 12.Qe2 Rb8 13.b3 … (−0.39 for you)`
- you lose the bishop pair
- b6 and d5 become holes in your camp (no pawn of yours can cover them)
- About king safety: Your king is safest behind pawns that haven't moved: each pawn you push in front of it stops guarding squares the enemy pieces can then use for a direct attack, and the pushed pawn itself becomes a target. Castle early, and move those pawns only for a concrete reason, not to stop a pin that isn't there yet. ([Lasker, Chess Strategy (Project Gutenberg \#5614), Chapter VI, The Middle Game: Breaking up the King's Side](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/5614))
- 5...e5 against 5...Nf6, in pawns: King safety −1.17, Piece placement −0.51, Piece activity −0.30, Threats +0.42
- Opening: B45 Sicilian Defense: Taimanov Variation
  - “1.e4 c5 2.Nf3 Nc6 3.Nc3 e6 Kasparov, Garry, & Keene, Raymond 1989 Batsford chess openings 2. ISBN 0-8050-3409-9.” ([Wikibooks, CC BY-SA 4.0](https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Chess_Opening_Theory/1._e4/1...c5/2._Nf3/2...Nc6/3._Nc3/3...e6))
- Sources: Stockfish 16, depth 20 · Stockfish 16 classical evaluation terms · [Edward Lasker, Chess Strategy (Project Gutenberg \#5614), Breaking up the King's Side](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/5614) (Public domain in the USA) · [lichess-org/chess-openings](https://github.com/lichess-org/chess-openings) (CC0) · [Wikibooks, CC BY-SA 4.0](https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Chess_Opening_Theory/1._e4/1...c5/2._Nf3/2...Nc6/3._Nc3/3...e6) (CC BY-SA 4.0, retrieved 2026-09-26)
- Review: [Win · 1+1 · vs 591 · 21 Sep](https://www.chess.com/game/live/184164572016) · [Game 2](https://www.chess.com/game/live/172076680058) · [Game 3](https://www.chess.com/game/live/164209701618)

### 3. 3...e6, better 3...cxd4

_Repeated mistake · 12% winning chances lost · you played it in 11 games_

- Position: After 1.e4 c5 2.Nf3 Nc6 3.d4. Red: your 3...e6, in 11 games (8 bullet, 3 blitz). Green: 3...cxd4, Stockfish's choice. `r1bqkbnr/pp1ppppp/2n5/2p5/3PP3/5N2/PPP2PPP/RNBQKB1R b KQkq - 0 3` · [Win · 1+1 · vs 636 · 25 Sep](https://www.chess.com/game/live/184368038032) · [analyse on Lichess](https://lichess.org/analysis/r1bqkbnr/pp1ppppp/2n5/2p5/3PP3/5N2/PPP2PPP/RNBQKB1R_b_KQkq_-_0_3?color=black)
  - Arrows: Your move e6 (red), better cxd4 (green)

After 3...e6, Stockfish's answer is 4.d5 Na5 5.d6 b6. Stockfish rates the line −1.82 for you, against −0.36 after 3...cxd4: a few moves later you stand 0.30 pawns worse on space and 0.28 on threats. Next time, ask whether your move gives your opponent room to expand.

- What 3...e6 allows: `3...e6 4.d5 Na5 5.d6 b6 6.Nc3 a6 7.e5 Bb7 8.Bd3 f5 9.Qe2 b5 10.Bg5 Qb6 11.Bxf5 … (−1.82 for you)`
- Better: 3...cxd4: `3...cxd4 4.Nxd4 e5 5.Nb5 d6 6.N1c3 Nf6 7.Bg5 a6 8.Na3 b5 9.Bxf6 gxf6 10.Nd5 f5 11.Bd3 … (−0.36 for you)`
- About space: Control of the centre gives you space: your pieces can switch quickly between the wings while your opponent's stay cramped behind their own pawns. Capablanca counts the four centre squares and notes that no violent attack succeeds without control of at least two of them. ([Capablanca, Chess Fundamentals (Project Gutenberg \#33870), Chapter I, First Principles: 7. Control of the Centre](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/33870))
- 3...e6 against 3...cxd4, in pawns: Space −0.30, Threats −0.28, Piece placement −0.22, Piece activity −0.21, Pawn structure +0.28
- Opening: B32 Sicilian Defense: Open
  - “White takes space in the centre. White invites Black to take the d-pawn.” ([Wikibooks, CC BY-SA 4.0](https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Chess_Opening_Theory/1._e4/1...c5/2._Nf3/2...Nc6/3._d4))

## Why these moves go wrong

The engine's lines for 3 positions from your games: what your move allowed, what was better, and the pattern to practise.

### 1. 2...Nc6, better 2...cxd4

_Repeated mistake · 12% winning chances lost · you played it in 17 games_

- Position: After 1.e4 c5 2.d4. Red: your 2...Nc6, in 17 games (9 bullet, 8 blitz). Green: 2...cxd4, Stockfish's choice. `rnbqkbnr/pp1ppppp/8/2p5/3PP3/8/PPP2PPP/RNBQKBNR b KQkq - 0 2` · [Loss · 3+2 · vs 900 · 31 Jul](https://www.chess.com/game/live/172314741072) · [analyse on Lichess](https://lichess.org/analysis/rnbqkbnr/pp1ppppp/8/2p5/3PP3/8/PPP2PPP/RNBQKBNR_b_KQkq_-_0_2?color=black)
  - Arrows: Your move Nc6 (red), better cxd4 (green)

After 2...Nc6, Stockfish's answer is 3.d5 Nb8 4.f4 g6. Stockfish rates the line −1.53 for you, against −0.19 after 2...cxd4: 3.d5 hits your knight with a pawn and it has to move again (3...Nb8), so you lose time; 2...cxd4 first takes the pawn that chases it. Next time, before you put a piece on a square, check whether a pawn can chase it away with a gain of time.

- What 2...Nc6 allows: `2...Nc6 3.d5 Nb8 4.f4 g6 5.Nc3 Bg7 6.Nf3 d6 7.a4 Bxc3+ 8.bxc3 Nf6 9.Qd3 O-O 10.Be2 … (−1.53 for you)`
- Better: 2...cxd4: `2...cxd4 3.Nf3 e5 4.c3 Nc6 5.cxd4 exd4 6.Nxd4 Nf6 7.Nc3 Bb4 8.Nxc6 bxc6 9.Bd3 d5 10.exd5 … (−0.19 for you)`
- About piece activity: A piece is worth what it can do: how many squares it reaches matters more than its point value. Put your pieces where they have the most scope, and look for ways to cut an enemy piece off from the fight; a bishop or knight shut out of play is almost a piece less. ([Lasker, Chess Strategy (Project Gutenberg \#5614), Chapter III, General Principles of Chess Strategy: Mobility](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/5614))
- 2...Nc6 against 2...cxd4, in pawns: King safety −0.84, Piece activity −0.43, Piece placement −0.33, Space −0.17, Knight placement −0.16, Threats +0.34
- Opening: B21 Sicilian Defense: Smith-Morra Gambit
  - “White opens up the centre immediately. This is an uncommon but tactical sideline to avoid the conventional Open Sicilian.” ([Wikibooks, CC BY-SA 4.0](https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Chess_Opening_Theory/1._e4/1...c5/2._d4))
- Sources: Stockfish 16, depth 20 · Stockfish 16 classical evaluation terms · [Edward Lasker, Chess Strategy (Project Gutenberg \#5614), Mobility](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/5614) (Public domain in the USA) · [José Raúl Capablanca, Chess Fundamentals (Project Gutenberg \#33870), 24. Cutting Off Pieces from the Scene of Action](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/33870) (Public domain in the USA) · [lichess-org/chess-openings](https://github.com/lichess-org/chess-openings) (CC0) · [Wikibooks, CC BY-SA 4.0](https://en.wikibooks.org/wiki/Chess_Opening_Theory/1._e4/1...c5/2._d4) (CC BY-SA 4.0, retrieved 2026-09-26)
- Review: [Loss · 3+2 · vs 900 · 31 Jul](https://www.chess.com/game/live/172314741072) · [Game 2](https://www.chess.com/game/live/174559149036) · [Game 3](https://www.chess.com/game/live/174003180048)

Green: 5...Nf6, Stockfish's choice. `r1bqkbnr/pp1p1ppp/2n1p3/8/2BNP3/8/PPP2PPP/RNBQK2R b KQkq - 0 5` · [Win · 10+0 · vs 1065 · 15 Feb 2025](https://www.chess.com/game/live/122917205436) · [analyse on Lichess](https://lichess.org/analysis/r1bqkbnr/pp1p1ppp/2n1p3/8/2BNP3/8/PPP2PPP/RNBQK2R_b_KQkq_-_0_5?color=black)
  - Arrows: Your move e5 (red), better Nf6 (green)

After 5...e5, Stockfish's answer is 6.Nf3 h6 7.Qd5 Qf6. Stockfish rates the line −1.08 for you, against +0.08 after 5...Nf6: White's queen gets to d5 (7.Qd5), a square no pawn of yours can cover, and a few moves later you stand 0.38 pawns worse on king safety. Next time, ask which squares in your camp no pawn of yours can cover any more, and which enemy piece can reach them.

- What 5...e5 allows: `5...e5 6.Nf3 h6 7.Qd5 Qf6 8.a3 d6 9.Nc3 Be6 10.Qd3 Bxc4 11.Qxc4 Qe6 12.Nd5 Rc8 13.Be3 … (−1.08 for you)`
- Better: 5...Nf6: `5...Nf6 6.Nc3 Qc7 7.Be2 Bc5 8.Nb3 Bb4 9.Qd3 O-O 10.Bg5 Qe5 11.Bd2 d5 (+0.08 for you)`
- d5 becomes a hole in your camp (no pawn of yours can cover it)
- About king safety: Your king is safest behind pawns that haven't moved: each pawn you push in front of it stops guarding squares the enemy pieces can then use for a direct attack, and the pushed pawn itself becomes a target. Castle early, and move those pawns only for a concrete reason, not to stop a pin that isn't there yet. ([Lasker, Chess Strategy (Project Gutenberg \#5614), Chapter VI, The Middle Game: Breaking up the King's Side](https://www.gutenberg.org/ebooks/5614))
- 5...e5 against 5...Nf6, in pawns: King safety −0.38, Piece activity −0.24, Piece placement −0.17, Pawn structure −0.16, Threats +0.49

### How this report works

- Every score is compared with what your rating predicts for each game (the Elo expected score for the rating gap), not with 50%. Beating lower-rated players is expected, so it is not counted as a strength. “+9 per 100 games vs your rating” means 9 game points more than your rating predicts in 100 games.
- A strength or weakness needs enough games (about 8 for an opening with one colour, 25 for splits such as games after a loss) and a significance test that allows for how many things were tested. On players without any real strengths or weaknesses, a report lists about 0.2 false claims on average.
- Observations (marked i) are patterns that did not pass that test, or plain facts: read them as context.
- Findings are associations, not proof of a cause: “you score less late at night” does not say why.
- The lists at the top show every strength and weakness, most important first (importance = how big the effect is × how sure we are); each finding is explained in its section.
- chess.com Game Review accuracy and this report's engine accuracy use different formulas. Compare each only with itself.
